           VAESEN OF CARPATHIA

       This chapter presents a selection of supernatural creatures from Central and Eastern Europe.
       The information in these pages is organized in the same way as in the Vaesen core rulebook. If you
       are a player, you should stop reading. The information that follows is for the Game­master only.




                                          BABA YAGA
People tell tales of an old woman living alone in the depths of the woods, her teeth made of iron
and her leg of bone. They say she had been there long before Christianity came to thes e lands.
They say one should not trust her sweet words while s eeking a deal with her.
   People tell tales of the old woman living alone in the depths of the woods… Now that
I have met her in person, I thank the Lord that among all the hags and witches, there is only
one Baba Yaga.
   People tell tales… None of them are true.
  —Antoine Augustin Calmet, April 1747

                                                  BABAI
It is so rare for adults to believe that a child’s fears can possibly be something bigger, more
s erious, and much more dangerous than just fairy tales and shadows in the dark corners.
Growing up, we los e something that allows us to understand—fairy tales are not just make
believe stories.
     When the children in one village started losing peaceful sleep and telling stories about a
crooked old man looking at them through the windows, adults believed their wild imagination
was to blame. Surely, they’d heard too many fairy tales and convinced thems elves they
 were s eeing things. But when some of thos e children went missing right from their homes
(at night!), I understood—there must be something behind thos e stories.
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, June 22, 1746

                                           DHAMPIR
When I passed through the Serbian villages in the northern Balkans, I heard about a
terrible monster that had recently s ettled in this region and attacked travelers and villagers.
All its victims were found bloodless. Having just heard this story, I was astonished to s ee a
tall man with a crossbow and a long knife on his belt enter the inn on a cold autumn evening.
The locals said that he was a hunter, and he humbly asked for my blessing before going into
the woods. While reading the prayer, I couldn’t take my eyes off the floor where his shadow
was suppos ed to be, but there was none. To bless him, I shook his gray hand, which appeared
highly elastic, and he went into the night. When we were preparing to leave in the morning,
he returned—dirty, with leaves and dried grass in his long black hair and his clothes covered
with traces of a dark liquid that res embled blood. He looked as if he had been in a terrible fight,
but without receiving a single scratch in the tumult. Despite his appearance, the locals greeted
him kindly and respectfully, inviting him to the table.
   “That beast won’t bother you anymore,” said the hunter and gave the innkeeper a dirty dark
bag with something round inside.
    We s et off, and I am still trying to understand what kind of trophy and from which beast
that strange hunter carried in the bag.
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, October 1734

                                            DREKAVAC
A couple of children wound up at the monastery’s doorstep—tired, dirty, and hungry.
They claimed to be the only survivors of the curs e that fell on a quaint little fishing hamlet some
two days away from here. After they were fed and had a good night’s sleep, I inquired more
about their misadventures. They told a story of a long chain of horrid accidents that had only
one thing in common—a soul-­numbing shriek that presaged each death.
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, August 1720

                      RITUA L
A drekavac can typically be driven out when the root
of the local tragedy is exposed, and locals are no lon-
ger distressed. For example, members of the Soci-
ety might solve the gruesome murder that initially
attracted the drekavac – the culprit gets sent to jail, and
people celebrate, chasing the shadows of tragedy away.
     Another way to expel a drekavac is to perform a com-
munal baptism at night. The number of participants
directly impacts the likelihood of a successful ritual.
     For temporary protection, one might also get a dog.
It is said that drekavaci hate dogs and try to avoid them.




                                                          · 43 ·
                                           · Vaesen of Carpathia ·




                                                GOLEM
When people feel afraid or frustrated, they often vent thes e emotions onto thos e who are weaker
and more vulnerable. That’s why orphans, widows, and strangers are often the first to be
blamed for all misfortunes.
    Once I witness ed what could have been a terrible pogrom when outsiders came willing to
burn, harm, and destroy the Jewish ghetto. But a quiet man, Loew was his name, stepped alone
against them. He said that if they refus ed to leave, they would regret it. The pogromist laughed
in Loew’s face and swung his club to hit him.
    Suddenly, a massive man of impossible strength jumped in to defend Loew. This large man
fought like a fierce beast, not a human being with a God-­given soul. No axe or club s eemed to
harm him. That threat of pogrom ended with the instigators running for their lives in panic.
    Loew never explained who defended him and the other Jews that night. A man? An angel
of the Lord? A devil’s offspring? Or something a human’s hand created? And the most
important—where has it gone?
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, June 1743

In Jewish folklore a golem is an artificial humanoid     persecution, or to help with simple or routine tasks.
created by a rabbi. The word “golem” means some-         The creator may shape a golem as they wish, depending
thing akin to “raw material” in Hebrew, a fitting name   on their skill, time, and the golem’s specific purpose.
for a being shaped from earth or clay and brought        However, golems are not always reliable and could

                                    HOMUNCULUS
Even the idea of creating a homunculus is disgusting and God-­defying. Created in sin by the
design of man, not God, and without a divine spark, this creature would be a soulless grim
s ervant, no more than a reflection of the sinful alchemist who would make such an attempt!
And yes, I say creature, since without a soul, it’s an empty husk inside which the Devil will
s et his plans.
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, January 1739

A homunculus is an artificial living being created by   as large as a human or even larger if the flask has
humans using magic and science. A creature is con-      enough space. Once the homunculus is out of the
sidered a homunculus if it was born without the par-    flask, it can no longer grow. Alchemists often use
ticipation of either God or a woman’s womb. Some        homunculi as their servants in secret laboratories,
homunculi can see into the future, while others can     hiding them from society when possible.
see and speak with spirits and
demons and share their pow-
ers with their master. Ambitious
alchemists all over the world try
to create homunculi, because
they have mystical powers and
secret knowledge that they can
share with their creators. But
some fear and despise such
ambition because those attempts
are already an act of defying God
by creating life without a soul.
    In the writings De natura rerum
(“The Nature of Things”), the
alchemist and physician Para-
celsus describes a procedure for
creating a homunculus. The
process involves maintaining a
man’s sperm at a warm tempera-
ture using decomposing horse
manure, allowing it to mature
within a sealed glass container
for a minimum of 40 days.
Once it has reached a grotesque
human fetus form, it must be
regularly fed with blood, and 40
weeks later, it will become a fully
developed homunculus. It can
be induced to continue to grow

                                    KHOKHA-YAROKHA
“That house wasn’t made by human hands, that’s for sure. There is hardly a man or a woman
 capable of doing such delicate and tiny work. There is hardly a man or a woman who would
 create a hous e and all its furniture out of teeth. Children’s teeth. Of cours e, this discovery
 attracted curiosity. Of cours e, I was among thos e who decided to go deep underground to
  s ee that in person. What a mistake it was! Now I shake every time I hear rustling and
 scraping in dark corners. They will find me. I know they will. They will come at night. They
 don’t like humans invading their home. They are going to take revenge. Oh my God, I’m sure I
  can hear them under the floor right now. I’m sure I can. They are coming for me!”
       Here the diary ends. The piece of paper looks as if somebody or something has bitten the rest
  of it with small but sharp teeth.
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, February 11, 1749

The khokha-­yarokha is a tiny vaesen with a lizard-­like                                RITUA L
body, covered with scales. However, its head does not               Khokha-­yarokhas can be talked to and convinced or
resemble that of a snake but rather a rat or a mouse                just physically killed. Anything that can kill a human
with long black whiskers and piercing sharp teeth.                  (other than fire) can kill a khokha-­yarokha.
Some may also have small wings.
    The khokha-­yarokha collects children’s baby teeth.
When a child loses a baby tooth, they need to leave it
on the floor for the night or drop it into a mouse hole
saying “Khokha-­yarokha, take this bone tooth and bring me a gold
one.” Some children also leave a piece of bread or pie
near their lost tooth. If this ritual is not performed, the
new tooth may not grow, or it will emerge sick and black.
    The khokha-­yarokha comes through the mouse
hole at night and picks up the tooth. It is said that
this vaesen’s house deep underground, as well as all
the furniture inside it, is built out of children’s teeth.

                     NACHZEHRER
             This happened in the summer when I visited
             Silesia to gather materials for my book.
              The city of Wrocław had recently suffered
              from the Great Plague, and there were many
              fresh graves at the local cemetery. Tadeusz,
              the cemetery keeper, was accompanying
             me when I heard terrible chewing sounds
             coming from one of the graves. When I
             looked down, I saw a coffin with a hole in
              it and the horrifying face of a monster who
             was loudly devouring its funeral shroud.
             Terrified, we fled the cemetery and reported it
             to the Church.
                 —Antoine Augustin Calmet, August 1739

             A nachzehrer is a German version of the vampire; its
             name consists of two parts: “nach,” meaning “after,”
             and “zehrer,” meaning “to eat” or “subsist on.” Insa-
             tiable appetite characterizes this undead. Its cause of
             death is often suicide or plague, and it was believed
             that when a plague swept a village, the first to suc-
             cumb to it would become a nachzehrer.
                The nachzehrer is driven by gluttonous hunger
             and primarily attacks its family. At night, it leaves the
             grave as a spirit and sucks the life out of its relatives.
                Its hunger is so great that it chews loudly even
             when lying in its coffin, which makes its grave easy to
             identify in the cemetery. Other signs of a nachzeh-
             rer’s grave are a hole bitten through the shroud and
             the body’s posture – it lies with one eye opened and
             holds its thumb firmly in the other hand. A nachzeh-
             rer can haunt its relatives only after it has consumed
                  its entire burial shroud.
                        Occasionally, the monster assumes its true
                        physical form, either to devour the flesh of
                          the corpses or to climb into the church
                            tower and ring the bell. Anyone who
                                 hears it is doomed to die before
                                  long. When a nachzehrer wants
                                    to move unnoticed, it assumes
                                      the form of a pig.

                                           RUSALKA
The first time I met a rusalka was in the area around the Shatsky Lakes. It was a hot
summer day and I saw a girl in a simple white dress sitting by the lake, combing her long
 green hair. As I approached to greet her, she turned to me. Suddenly I realized she wasn’t
human—she had bluish skin and her eyes blazed with green fire. I reached frantically for my
garlic, which I always carried with me on my travels in Eastern Europe. But the woman just
smiled at me, jumped gracefully into the lake, and disappeared without a trace.
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, July 1746

                                                STRIGOI
The priest, nervously wiping sweat from his forehead, recounted to me the events of that terrible
night and the following years, deeply rooted in the memories of locals, both old and young.
Allegedly, a man named Yure was s een on the village streets after his funeral. Marred by death,
he looked alive but terrifying. The pale skin of his face was tightly stretched over the skull, torn
apart by a wide, ominous mouth. The undead monster visited his former wife, neighbors, and
other residents on moonless nights. Such meetings with Yure usually meant death. This plague
continued for a terrible sixteen years. The horror ended when a few daredevils put his curs ed
soul to rest.
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, December 1734

                                  UDILNYTSIA
I visited this man in a local asylum, and he looked just like a person who should be there:
wild eyes, messy hair, murmuring something… It was difficult to believe that once he had been
a doctor, a man of science, calm, resourceful, clever, quite famous, and perfectly sane. I caught
myself wondering what could lead such a bright personality to such a horrible state. I tried
talking to him and asking questions, but I don’t think he understood a single word. So, I
just wrote down everything I was able to understand in his muttering. Again and again, he
mentioned a woman with a knife that didn’t leave any wounds. Poor lunatic!
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, May 16, 1750
                                                   An udilnytsia is an evil female spirit, a thief of talents,
                                                   fate, and good luck. They can prey upon any unpro-
                                                   tected person but prefer pregnant women and their
                                                   unborn babies. If the pregnant woman does not wear
                                                   any protective amulets – such as a cross, holy water, or
                                                                       a shirt with special embroideries
                                                                             – and the house has unpro-
                                                                                tected windows, doors, or
                                                                                   chimney, an udilnytsia
                                                                                    can sneak inside.
                                                                                          They use magical
                                                                                       knives, made from
                                                                                        the bones of an
                                                                                        unborn child, to
                                                                                        cut the womb and
                                                                                        take an unborn
                                                                                       baby. The udilnyt-
                                                                                      sia sucks all the tal-
                                                                                    ent and all the fate out
                                                                                  of the baby, and then
                                                                                returns it to the mother’s
                                                                             body, using the same knife to
                                                                       heal the cut and leave no marks.
                                                   Such a baby is born sickly and crippled, if it is born at
                                                   all. Sometimes an udilnytsia may take a baby for itself.
                                                       Udilnytsias looks like pale women with long,
                                                   messy dark hair hiding their faces. Their skinny,
                                                   long hands have sharp nails (or rather, claws). They
                                                   wear rags instead of clothes. Udilnytsias like being
                                                   together with others of their kind. They gather in
                                                   abandoned places to party and chat, sometimes
                                                   turning into magpies.

                                                 VAMPIR
While traveling from the north Balkans to Bucharest, I heard of and recorded different local
 traditions of burial, which had been strangely adapted to the Christian tradition. People of
 this land are cautious with the recently deceas ed, especially if they had a dubious reputation in
life. Bodies are guarded so no cat, dog, human, or other living thing moves over them. Others
are burying corps es face down, binding their hands and legs with rope, impaling bodies in their
coffins through the torso or head with an ash, hawthorn, or aspen stake or spike, and driving
similar wooden spikes into the grave after the burial.
     The most drastic measures that I heard of were decapitation of the corps e and placing the
 head beneath the coffin, removing the heart and boiling it in wine, and complete cremation of the
 body, which is very difficult. All thes e measures are applied depending on the demeanor of the
  deceas ed. There is special caution for thos e who were the s eventh sons, for children born with
witch signs such as milk teeth, tails, or third nipples, or for thos e who had been criminals,
suicide victims, wizards, and witches. Such rituals are believed to prevent people from returning
 from death as vampires, bloodthirsty immortal beings that will doom their former communities.
    —Antoine Augustin Calmet, November 1734

                                          VÂRCOLAC
The most engrossing event I witnessed was during the longest night in the winter. I stayed at a
small village on my way to Bran. Looking out the window, I was charmed by snow shining
in the light of a beautiful full moon. It was so bright outside that I could s ee the whole
village. Suddenly, I saw a color wave go through hous es and streets, changing everything
from moon-­white into blood-­red. The owner of the place and all the locals that were inside
 needed just a second to understand. They simultaneously grabbed the first things they could find
and rushed outside, banging on pans and pots. A few moments later, the church bell joined
that noise with a hysterical rhythm, followed by gunfire. It was a bizarre picture, watching
as people filled the streets, making nois e with everything they had in this red light of a bloody
moon. It took ten minutes until the white light returned, going through the village in a wave,
like nothing happened. The celebration lasted all night. People were singing and drinking in
honor of saving the moon from a beast.
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, January 1735

                                           VODIANYK
To learn more about the undead, I delved into stories about where they come from. Over and
over, I heard about one spirit in particular that is not undead by nature but somehow rais es
and rules over drowned ones—the Vodianyk. All through my journey, fishermen warned me
that the Lord of the Rivers is compelling and can grant wishes, but it’s dangerous to even listen
 to his words because they are full of lies, and it is mortally dangerous to accept his offers.
Almost every fisherman to the east of the San pays their fee and releas es their first catch back
 into the river—as their way of paying respect to traditions founded by Vodianyk. I wish I
 could encounter this creature in my future travels.
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, July 1746

more powerful Vodianyk is. He resembles a green-­                 EX A MPLES OF CONFLICTS

                                          VYKHROVYK
When I journeyed to the Carpathian Mountains to study the local undead spirits, I noticed
that Oleksa, my Hutsul companion who showed me the way, always carried a strange stick
with him. It was made of elder wood and had a copper head in the shape of a horrible
monster who res embled a winged old man with his insides out. Oleksa said that this stick
protected us from the vykhrovyks, undead air spirits, and was made in their likeness.
   —Antoine Augustin Calmet, October 1749

Vykhrovyks are Slavic spirits of air and whirlwinds, born                            RITUA L

                                       ZERKALYTSIA
My studies of the undead often led me to funerals and cemeteries. Once, I was pres ent at the
funeral of an old fortune-­teller in Galicia. One ritual in particular attracted my attention:
covering all the mirrors in the hous e with shawls when the coffin was carried out. After the
funeral, the locals explained to me that this is necessary so that the soul of the deceas ed does not
get into the mirror and become a zerkalytsia. To all my questions about it, they answered that it
 is better not to look into some mirrors in the dark.
    —Antoine Augustin Calmet, February 1737

· Bite Marks: All children have distinctive scars on
  their necks, marks left after the knez’s repeated
    feeding and healing of the wound. The children
    typically cover the wounds with high collars, but
    they may be spotted with an INVESTIGATION test. If
    confronted about this when the knez is not present,
    a MANIPULATION test will have the children admit
    the cause of the bite marks.

     JOSEF STR ASSER, WA RDEN

                                                                  · 106 ·
              PRISONERS
         “Something bad is happening, but
            who cares if a few of us die?”

There are hundreds of prisoners in Brygidki,
many of them accused of serious crimes. The
player characters will notice at first glance
that the prison is overpopulated. There are
many more prisoners in each cell than there
are beds – some of them sleep on thin mat-
tresses laid on the cold floor.
    Most of the prisoners are in poor health,
rarely getting to walk outside their cells, and
are treated harshly by the prison’s staff. They
are afraid of what could happen to them and
feel that no one in this prison will defend
their lives. Many prisoners believe in the
Angel of Justice – Father Hryhorii’s explana-
tion for what is happening. They try to con-
vince the guards to let them attend a sermon                            I VA N FR A NKO
so that they can confess and repent for their                  “Do you have a pencil to spare, by any chance?”
sins to protect themselves from God’s wrath.

                                                THE SECRET
                                             OF THE MAHARAL

              This mystery takes the player characters to 19th                 indicating the use of enormous force. One might
              century Prague – a city of many cultures and, in a               even say, unnaturally great force. People of differ-
              way, a city of many cities, consisting of several diverse        ent communities are being killed, triggering fears of
              urban entities merged together.                                  religious unrest. All the victims have only one thing
                                                                               in common – they were killed at night.
                                                                                   Virtually helpless, the city police are turning to

                                             PRELUDE                           various associations and groups – including kabbal-
                                                                               ists, spiritualists, and experts in ancient cultures and
                                      “People are dying in Prague.”            beliefs – for help. The city is on the verge of declaring
                                                                               a state of emergency and sending in the army – after
              Such information may not have been unusual – after               all, riots could break out at any moment. Some think

        To The Honorable Pravda Society,
        I am writing this letter on behalf of all the people of Bralnic Valley,
        whom I humbly serve as Knez Vlad the Tenth. My castle and the
        valley are located among the beautiful Carpathians in Transylvania,
        far from the worries of the Empire.
        Just as we prepare for our yearly celebrations of my forefather Knez Vlad
        the First, our peaceful life has been suddenly interrupted by an
         unknown beast. It has brutally killed two farmers, and we found their
        bloodless and heartless bodies. Only God knows how many more will
        become the beast’s prey. Our best hunters, including myself, cannot
         establish the nature of the beast, nor can we find its tracks or lair
                                                                             .
        My grandfather, the Knez Vlad the Eighth, was honored to have
        members of your Society visit our castle. He witnessed their remarkable
        knowledge and craftsmanship and described it in his diary, which
        I found while exploring the library for clues on the beast. As soon as
        I read about the Society, I wrote this letter with confidence that your
        organization still exists in peace and prosperity.
        I know the road to my domain is long and may be tiring, but
        my children in Europe will arrange everything they can to make it
        comfortable for you. You will be guests of honor in my castle, and I will
        provide you with generous remuneration for your services. I have no
        doubts that your visit will help our valley and become the beginning of
        our long-­lasting friendship.
        Please follow the itinerary I have prepared for you. As mentioned
        above, my children will make all necessary reservations for the Society.
        With anticipation of your visit  ,
             Sincerely,
                  Knez Vlad the Tenth

        To whom it may concern,

         My name is Josef Strasser. I am the Warden of
         Brygidki prison in the city of Lemberg, in the
         western part of Galicia. Lately, some unusual events
         have occurred here – several prisoners fell ill, and
         some of them have died rather gruesome deaths since
         then. I fear this streak might continue, and the
         thought that I can’t do anything to help people who are
         my responsibility haunts me.

         I believe in the safety of my prison, and those deaths
         looked out of the ordinary. I am no expert on the
         supernatural and, therefore, I am in great need of
         your assistance.

         Here is a list of things that are not allowed within
         the Brygidki prison:

        - explosives or weapons of any kinds,
        - anything sharp, like knives or scissors,
        - tobacco or any products containing it,
        - alcohol or any drinks/food containing it,
        - money or any equivalent in valuables,
        - medicine or drugs,
        - lighters or matches.

        If you are able and willing to intervene in this
        matter, your attention will be greatly appreciated.
        As lives are endangered, time is of the essence.

         Most respectfully,
         Josef Strasser

              as for a wandering vengeful spirit, com-
              monly known in these lands as a ­wietrznica
              or vykhrovyk, only one way to stop it is
              known. But a knife you will need. As no
              easy ways to hurt the wind exists, the one
              fighting a vykhrovyk requires something
              more than just steel. You must take a knife
              and find a church. Enter the Lord’s house
              and speak politely to His servants. Then
              make your request. Let them take the knife
              you wield and put it in the holy water. Let
              them pray again and again until the knife of
              yours becomes the Lord’s sword. Only then
              can you fight against this vengeful spirit. As
              your eyes see it, throw the knife, and it will
              pin the Devil’s creature to the ground.

        Finding my ancestor’s notes was so
        reinvigorating. It might be the
        breakthrough I was looking for.
        Our nation, as well as all Slavic
        nations, can benefit from this.
        The voice of the oppressed will finally
        be heard. I can’t believe that I had
        this marvelous solution so close for so
        many years. I must study it further
        to understand what can be done for our
        cause.

        ancestor was so vile that he used this
        power for such petty things. It is
        almost unbelievable. But my work
        will correct that. It will be a
        magnificent creation that benefits all
        people. It will be used for the greater
        good, not the Jews or the Czechs only,
        but for all those who for too long lived
        under the boot of tyranny. I ’m eager
        to complete my work.

                         Venture into
                  Mythic Carpathia to face
                 Vampires, Rusalkas, Baba
               Yaga, and other even stranger
              horrors of Central and Eastern
             European folklore. Wander the busy
              streets of Prague, where history
                and myths collide. Visit old
                cities, deep forests and high
                 mountains to face creatures
                        beyond belief.



This beautifully illustrated hardback expansion to the award-
winning Vaesen – Nordic Horror Roleplaying includes information
about the Carpathia branch of the Society, a gazetteer of the city of
Prague, a chapter with local vaesen, and three complete mysteries
to play. Mythic Carpathia is illustrated by the acclaimed artists Johan
Egerkrans, Anton Vitus, and Francesca Baerald.

Key features:
 ·Information about the Carpathian Society called “Pravda,”
  its founders, and headquarters in Prague.
 ·The new archetype Vampire Hunter.
 ·A chapter detailing no less than 16 local mythical beings,
  each with background, stats, and story seeds. These include
  Vampire, Golem, the infamous Baba Yaga, and many more.
 ·Three new complete mysteries to play, set in Prague, Ukraine,
  and Transylvania.
 ·A large separate full-color map of Mythic Carpathia and the
  city of Prague.
